=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer array nums of length n and a 2D array queries, where queries[i] = [l_i, r_i, val_i].
Each queries[i] represents the following action on nums:

Select a subset of indices in the range [l_i, r_i] from nums.
Decrement the value at each selected index by exactly val_i.

A Zero Array is an array with all its elements equal to 0.
Return the minimum possible non-negative value of k, such that after processing the first k queries in sequence, nums becomes a Zero Array. If no such k exists, return -1.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [2,0,2], queries = [[0,2,1],[0,2,1],[1,1,3]]
Output: 2
Explanation:

For query 0 (l = 0, r = 2, val = 1):

Decrement the values at indices [0, 2] by 1.
The array will become [1, 0, 1].


For query 1 (l = 0, r = 2, val = 1):

Decrement the values at indices [0, 2] by 1.
The array will become [0, 0, 0], which is a Zero Array. Therefore, the minimum value of k is 2.




Example 2:

Input: nums = [4,3,2,1], queries = [[1,3,2],[0,2,1]]
Output: -1
Explanation:
It is impossible to make nums a Zero Array even after all the queries.

Example 3:

Input: nums = [1,2,3,2,1], queries = [[0,1,1],[1,2,1],[2,3,2],[3,4,1],[4,4,1]]
Output: 4
Explanation:

For query 0 (l = 0, r = 1, val = 1):

Decrement the values at indices [0, 1] by 1.
The array will become [0, 1, 3, 2, 1].


For query 1 (l = 1, r = 2, val = 1):

Decrement the values at indices [1, 2] by 1.
The array will become [0, 0, 2, 2, 1].


For query 2 (l = 2, r = 3, val = 2):

Decrement the values at indices [2, 3] by 2.
The array will become [0, 0, 0, 0, 1].


For query 3 (l = 3, r = 4, val = 1):

Decrement the value at index 4 by 1.
The array will become [0, 0, 0, 0, 0]. Therefore, the minimum value of k is 4.




Example 4:

Input: nums = [1,2,3,2,6], queries = [[0,1,1],[0,2,1],[1,4,2],[4,4,4],[3,4,1],[4,4,5]]
Output: 4

 
Constraints:

1 <= nums.length <= 10
0 <= nums[i] <= 1000
1 <= queries.length <= 1000
queries[i] = [l_i, r_i, val_i]
0 <= l_i <= r_i < nums.length
1 <= val_i <= 10

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def minZeroArray(self, nums: List[int], queries: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

We can return 0. Simpler code:

```
class Solution:
    def minZeroArray(self, nums: List[int], queries: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        dp = [1] * n
        if all(x == 0 for x in nums):
            return 0
        for k, (l,r,v) in enumerate(queries, 1):
            for i in range(l, r+1):
                dp[i] |= dp[i] << v
            if all(((dp[i] >> nums[i]) & 1) for i in range(n)):
                return k
        return -1
```

Let's maybe prove sufficiency more formally for ourselves:
Given DP, for each i, there is a subset S_i of queries among first k with sum A_i. For each query j, define T_j = { i | j ∈ S_i }. Since j ∈ S_i only if i in [l_j,r_j], T_j is subset of range. Apply query j to T_j. For each i, total decrement = sum_{j: i∈T_j} v_j = sum_{j∈S_i} v_j = A_i. Since decrements are nonnegative and total equals initial, final zero and no intermediate negative. Thus feasible. Necessity: If sequence makes zero, for each i let S_i be queries where i selected. Sum v_j = A_i because final zero and no negative? If no negative, yes. If negative intermediate impossible final zero. Thus DP condition. Good.

Let's maybe include code:

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minZeroArray(self, nums: List[int], queries: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        dp = [1] * n
        if all(x == 0 for x in nums):
            return 0

        for k, (l, r, v) in enumerate(queries, 1):
            for i in range(l, r + 1):
                dp[i] |= dp[i] << v
            if all((dp[i] >> nums[i]) & 1 for i in range(n)):
                return k
        return -1
```

Would `dp[i] |= dp[i] << v` be okay if `dp[i]` is huge? yes. But note in Python, augmented assignment `|=` on list item: It will get item, perform operation, set item. The right side `dp[i] << v` uses current `dp[i]`; left side also current. No issue. To be extra clear:
```
dp[i] = dp[i] | (dp[i] << v)
```
Let's use that to avoid any potential confusion. It reads dp[i] twice but same. Good.

Now, let's think if we should mask dp to avoid unbounded growth? Not necessary. But maybe if q=1000, val=10, sum=10000, int bit length 10001, trivial. If nums[i] max 1000, we could mask to `(1 << (nums[i]+1)) - 1` to keep small, but if nums[i]=0, mask 1, then adding future values? If already achieved, no need. But if we mask, subset sums beyond nums[i] are not needed because they can never be used to achieve nums[i] (positive vals only). Actually if a subset sum exceeds nums[i], adding more positive values cannot bring it down, so we can discard sums > nums[i] for each index. We could mask to `(1 << (nums[i] + 1)) - 1` to keep bits small. But if nums[i]=0, mask=1, and dp[i] remains 1. That's fine. If we mask, we still need to update for other indices? For index i already achieved, no need. But if we mask globally, for an index with nums[i]=1000, max bit 1000. This is safe and more efficient. However, if we mask dp[i] to nums[i], then `dp[i] |= dp[i] << v` and then `dp[i] &= mask`. This prevents growth. But not necessary. Could include for neatness:
```
mask = (1 << (nums[i] + 1)) - 1
dp[i] = (dp[i] | (dp[i] << v)) & mask
```
But if nums[i]=0, mask=1, dp stays1. If nums[i] large, fine. But if nums[i] = 0 and we mask to1, then later queries don't create bit0? bit0 remains. Good. If nums[i] = 5, mask bits0..5. We only care bit5. This is safe because any subset sum >5 cannot be reduced by later positive queries to exactly5. So we can discard. This reduces bit length to <=1001. But code with mask inside loop needs compute mask for each index. We can precompute masks = [(1 << (x+1))-1 for x in nums]. Then update:
```
dp[i] = (dp[i] | (dp[i] << v)) & masks[i]
```
This is still simple. But if nums[i]=0, mask=1, dp[i]|=dp[i]<<v =1|2=3, &1=1. Good. If nums[i]=1000, mask 2^1001-1. Good.

Should we include mask? It might be slightly more code but robust. Without mask, dp bits up to 10000, still fine. With mask, if nums[i]=0, we still update all covered indices but dp remains1. No issue. Let's decide. Simpler without mask is acceptable. But if nums[i] can be 0 and dp[i] grows to huge, still trivial. q=1000, n=10, bit length 10000, not huge. I'd keep no mask for clarity. But maybe if q=1000, val=10, sum=10000, bit length 10001, Python int operations still very fast. No problem.

However, if we don't mask, for nums[i]=0, dp[i] will accumulate all subset sums of covering queries, but bit0 remains. All check for zero index always true. Fine.

Let's think if there is a need to process queries in sequence and "minimum k" maybe if answer is found at k, but our DP includes all queries up to k; if a query has interval covering i and val v, adding it to DP corresponds to either selecting i or not. Yes.

Now, let's consider if there is a more subtle coupling: Suppose query j is selected for index i and index h. In our construction, T_j = {i,h}. But what if for index i, S_i includes j, and for index h, S_h includes j, but the actual operation "Select a subset of indices in the range" allows selecting both. Yes. What if S_i includes j for all i in range, T_j = full range. Allowed. Good.

Now, let's think about if a query can be selected for an index even if that index's current value is not enough at that time due to other indices? No, current value per index independent. We proved.

n=2, nums=[2,3], queries:

Let's decide final code. The simplest robust:
```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minZeroArray(self, nums: List[int], queries: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        dp = [1] * n
        if all(x == 0 for x in nums):
            return 0

        for k, (l, r, v) in enumerate(queries, 1):
            for i in range(l, r + 1):
                dp[i] |= dp[i] << v
            if all((dp[i] >> nums[i]) & 1 for i in range(n)):
                return k
        return -1
```
This is very short. But note if nums all zero, return0. If not all zero, zero indices dp[zero]=1 and all check true. Good.

Could there be a problem with `dp[i] |= dp[i] << v` if `dp[i]` is a Python int and we are updating in place? No.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "Decrement the value at each selected index by exactly val_i" and if val_i is positive; yes.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "nums becomes a Zero Array" after processing first k queries, but if an index is decremented by a total equal to initial, final zero. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "minimum possible non-negative value of k" if answer is 0 and nums all zero; yes.


</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minZeroArray(self, nums: List[int], queries: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        dp = [1] * n  # bit i is set if sum i is achievable

        if all(x == 0 for x in nums):
            return 0

        for k, (l, r, v) in enumerate(queries, 1):
            for i in range(l, r + 1):
                dp[i] = dp[i] | (dp[i] << v)

            if all((dp[i] >> nums[i]) & 1 for i in range(n)):
                return k

        return -1
```
